See, e.g., Rompilla, 545 U.S. at 390-93, 125 S.Ct. 2456 (granting relief where counsel failed to investigate and present evidence of petitioner’s abusive and neglectful childhood and petitioner’s organic brain damage); Williams (Terry), 529 U.S. at 395-99, 120 S.Ct. 1495 (finding ineffectiveness where counsel failed to investigate and present graphic evidence of petitioner’s nightmarish childhood, including severe beatings and foster care while petitioner’s parents served prison terms for criminal neglecddt); Stankewitz v. Woodford, 365 F.3d 706, 723 (9th Cir.2004) (granting evidentiary hearing on claim of IAC where counsel failed to investigate and present “an excess of privation and abuses” experienced by petitioner as a child, including severe beatings, foster care and organic brain damage to the point of borderline mental retardation); Ainsworth, 268 F.3d at 874-78 (granting petition where counsel failed to investigate, develop and present evidence of petitioner’s troubled background and emotional instability); Wallace v. Stewart, 184 F.3d 1112, 1115-16 (9th Cir.1999) (granting petition where counsel conducted no factual investigation into petitioner’s very dysfunctional background); Seidel v. Merkle, 146 F.3d 750, 756 (9th Cir.1998) (granting petition upon counsel’s failure to investigate and present a mental illness defense at penalty); compare Campbell v. Kincheloe, 829 F.2d 1453 (9th Cir.1987) (finding counsel’s decision not to present mitigating evidence reasonable and, assuming deficiency, no prejudice). While petitioner has not demonstrated prejudice sufficient to undermine confidence in the penalty verdict, the Court will consider counsel’s deficiencies in the cumulative error analysis.
Lastly, petitioner alleges as part of Claim 5(C)(9) that trial counsel failed to call a youth authority counselor to testify that petitioner had not been dangerous when previously incarcerated. Both Dr. Girsh and Dr. Maloney testified that petitioner likely would do well while incarcerated. Evidence from an additional person would have been cumulative and may have opened the door to negative evidence about petitioner’s juvenile violations. Counsel was not deficient, nor did his alleged deficiency cause petitioner prejudice.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claims 5(C)(1), 5(C)(2), 5(C)(4) and 5(C)(9) but will consider counsel’s deficiencies in the cumulative error analysis.
6. Counsel’s failure to investigate and present evidence of petitioner’s neurological impairment and to obtain a complete social and medical history of petitioner (Claims 5(C)(7) & 5(D)(32))
In Claim 5(C)(7), petitioner contends that trial counsel failed to investigate and develop evidence of petitioner’s neurological and mental impairment, including by failing to use petitioner’s expert witnesses effectively on the issue of petitioner’s mental state at the time of the offenses. In Claim 5(D)(32), petitioner asserts that trial counsel inadequately prepared for petitioner’s defense. Counsel’s alleged shortcomings include: (1) failure to retain a social historian; (2) failure to obtain a complete medical history or neurological examination of petitioner; (3) failure to consult with experts about the impact of (a), physical, sexual and emotional abuse and neglect, (b) drug and alcohol abuse (c) petitioner’s father’s views about racism and (d) being an adopted child of mixed-race heritage; and (4) failure to provide all relevant information to experts.
a. Deficiency
As discussed, some evidence in the record may have suggested to counsel that a neurological examination of petitioner was unnecessary. Every expert consulted found that petitioner had the capacity to commit rape and murder. Dr. Girsh diagnosed petitioner with antisocial personality disorder but also testified that petitioner might meet the criteria for antisocial personality disorder.
At the same time, Dr. Girsh also told counsel before trial that there was some indication of an organic, neurological basis for petitioner’s behavior and that petitioner suffered from dyslexia. (9 CDD at P00427 (Exh. L-7)). Dr. Maloney told counsel that petitioner had been psychotic, that petitioner suffered from potentially serious psychological problems and that he had a highly pathological profile. (9 CDD at P00738 (Exh. L-5); JTD P00829.) Ultimately, Dr. Maloney testified at penalty that nothing suggested that petitioner lacked the capacity to commit the crimes. (14 RT 3473.)
Ample data did exist, however, that was reasonably available to counsel and that may have influenced both counsel’s approach to trial and the experts’ evaluations of petitioner. Counsel failed to retain a social historian or to otherwise investigate petitioner’s background, and he also failed to obtain petitioner’s complete medical history. Due to counsel’s failure to conduct basic investigation, none of the experts who evaluated petitioner before trial reviewed records regarding petitioner’s birth family, or social history information from petitioner’s adopted family, preschool teacher and others. Dr. Maloney and Dr. Girsh knew that petitioner had been referred for a neurological examination at age five, that he had performed poorly on psychological tests and that he engaged in behavior suggesting neurological or psychiatric problems during his preschool years. However, none of the experts knew the following key information: that petitioner inherited a genetic vulnerability to mental illness; that he was born to a 14-year-old mother who abused drugs and alcohol during her pregnancy and who was in a violent relationship; that petitioner’s birth father was unemployed, incarcerated and suffered from serious mental illness; that petitioner was delivered with the help of forceps, which are known to cause brain injury; that petitioner suffered many head injuries as a child; that petitioner’s preschool teacher was very concerned about petitioner and his home life; that petitioner was' described as a troubled and hyperactive child; that petitioner had endured the forcible administration of enemas and other abuse at the hands of his adoptive parents; or that petitioner was largely neglected by both his parents. In addition, various indicators suggested that petitioner suffered from neuropsychiatric impairments. .(Gur 6/8/04 Decl. at 4, ¶ 9.) Petitioner’s upbringing included child abuse and neglect, both psychological and physical, which can lead to brain damage or dissociative disorders. (Id. at 5, ¶9.) Petitioner also had poor impulse control and poor scholastic performance despite normal IQ scores. (Id.)
“Counsel has a duty to make reasonable investigations or to make a reasonable decision that makes particular investigations unnecessary.” Strickland, 466 U.S. at 691, 104 S.Ct. 2052. “In assessing counsel’s investigation, [the Court must] conduct an objective review of [his] performance, measured for reasonableness under prevailing professional norms, which includes a context-dependent consideration of the challenged conduct as seen from counsel’s perspective at the time.” Wiggins v. Smith, 539 U.S. at 523, 123 S.Ct. 2527. .
Here, “[e]ounsel’s failure to investigate [petitioner’s] mental condition cannot be construed as a trial tactic.” Evans v. Lewis, 855 F.2d 631 (9th Cir.1988). At the time of petitioner’s trial, which took place in 1983, competent counsel had a duty to investigate a defendant’s background. See, e.g., Penny, 492 U.S. at 319, 109 S.Ct. 2934 (holding, in reviewing a case tried in March of 1980, that “evidence about the defendant’s background and character is relevant because of the belief, long held by this society, that defendants who commit criminal acts that are attributable to a disadvantaged background, or to emotional or mental problems, may be less culpable than defendants who have no such excuse”), overruled on other grounds in Atkins v. Virginia, 536 U.S. 304 at 321, 122 S.Ct. 2242, 153 L.Ed.2d 335 (2002); Bean, 163 F.3d at 1080 (“[W]e have previously recognized an attorney’s duty to investigate and present mitigating evidence of mental impairment in the context of a 1979 capital sentencing hearing.”) (citing Evans v. Lewis, 855 F.2d 631, 636-37 (9th Cir.1988).); Cf. Bean, 163 F.3d at 1080 (“[T]he ineffectiveness at issue in this case did not arise from failure to employ novel or neoteric tactics. Rather, it resulted from inadequacies in rudimentary trial preparation and presentation: providing experts with requested information, performing recommended testing, conducting adequate investigation, and preparing witnesses for trial testimony. These were not alien concepts in 1981, but were an integral thread in the fabric of constitutionally effective representation.”)
Counsel’s failure to investigate petitioner’s background and his medical history were not sound trial strategy. See, e.g., Williams (Terry), 529 U.S. at 395-99, 120 S.Ct. 1495 (holding that counsel’s failure to investigate and present evidence of defendant’s mental defect and social history constitutes deficient performance); Caro v. Woodford, 280 F.3d 1247, 1254 (9th Cir.2002) (“[C]ounsel’s failure to investigate and provide appropriate experts with the information necessary to evaluate [petitioner’s] neurological system for mitigation constituted deficient performance within the meaning of Strickland.”); see also Frierson v. Woodford, 463 F.3d 982, 989 (9th Cir.2006) (“The imperative to cast a wide net for all relevant mitigating evidence is heightened at a capital sentencing hearing because ‘the Constitution prohibits imposition of the death penalty without adequate consideration of factors which might evoke mercy.’ ”) (quoting Caro, 165 F.3d at 1227).
Moreover, counsel relied in part on a clinical psychologist retained by prior counsel. While trial counsel did retain an additional clinical psychologist, he failed to conduct enough investigation to make an informed decision about what sorts of other experts to consult or about what information to give the experts he did retain. Counsel has an obligation to conduct an investigation that will allow counsel to figure out what sort of experts to consult, and then counsel must present those experts with relevant information. Caro, 165 F.3d at 1226-27. Counsel’s inadequate investigation resulted in counsel failing to consult a neurologist, neuropsychologist or neuropsychiatrist, or to arrange for a neurological examination of petitioner, despite information in counsel’s file suggesting the utility of such an approach. Counsel had no tactical reason for not requesting a neurological examination. (1 CDD 34; see also 1 CDD 34-35 (“Evidence of neurological impairment is the type of evidence I wanted because it would have helped to explain and mitigate Francis’[s] state of mind at the time of the killings.”)) Counsel owed a duty to locate experts who could explain medical or neurological issues to the jury. For example,
Although the defendant in Caro was examined by four experts prior to trial, including a medical doctor, a psychologist, and a psychiatrist, none of whom indicated that the defendant suffered from mental impairment severe enough to constitute diminished capacity, [the Ninth Circuit] concluded that defense counsel was ineffective for failing to consult an appropriate expert with expertise in neurology or toxicology. [Petitioner] had been exposed to high levels of toxic chemicals and pesticides as a child, and because none of the experts were neurologists or toxicologists, none was able to conduct the neurological testing needed to evaluate the effects that the pesticides and chemicals had on [petitioner’s] brain.
Frierson, 463 F.3d at 992 (internal quotation marks omitted). Likewise, petitioner was exposed to alcohol, drugs and violence in útero; was delivered with the help of forceps; suffered many accidents as a child, many of them involving head trauma; was abused by his parents; and began abusing alcohol and drugs in elementary school. Also, readily available information that counsel could have obtained through an adequate investigation would have raised additional questions about whether petitioner suffered from neurological problems. -None of the experts was capable of conducting the neurological testing necessary to evaluate the effects of these circumstances on petitioner’s brain. Counsel performed deficiently. See Kenley v. Armontrout, 937 F.2d 1298, 1304 (8th Cir.1991) (“Failing to interview witnesses or discover mitigating evidence relates to trial preparation and not trial strategy.”); cf. Bean v. Calderon, 163 F.3d at 1079 (Counsel’s “presentation of the[] mental health experts’ testimony did not arise from the- requisite informed judgment.”) (internal. quotation marks omitted). Counsel also failed to elicit testimony at trial regarding the impact of Naomi’s schizophrenia on petitioner. (See, supra, Discussion of Claims 5(B)(3)(c) & 5(C)(3)). Moreover, although counsel testified that he knew petitioner had been abused by his parents, counsel apparently did not explore this issue with his experts or elicit testimony regarding the abuse at the penalty phase.
b. Guilt phase prejudice
Petitioner argues that had counsel investigated petitioner’s mental and neurological impairments and conducted a social history, then counsel could have presented a guilt phase defense that petitioner was unable to form the requisite intents. Petitioner has failed to show guilt phase prejudice.
Petitioner suffers from brain damage that is likely organic, meaning that it was caused by a head injury, either in útero, later or both. (Gur 2/8/05 Decl. at 14, ¶ 27.) Petitioner’s mother abused substances and was in a violent relationship during her pregnancy, petitioner was delivered with the help of forceps and petitioner was involved in many incidents of head trauma as a child, adolescent and teen. (Id.) Petitioner’s congenital brain damage probably was complicated by the home life in which he was raised. (Id. at 10, ¶ 18.) The brain damage affects various regions of petitioner’s brain and causes petitioner to struggle with verbal memory impairment and interpreting emotional information, both of which are exacerbated by extreme emotion or stress. (Id. at 10-11, ¶ 18-20.)
Neuropsychological tests available in 1982 and 1983 would have uncovered these impairments, but no one conducted a neuropsychological evaluation of petitioner at the time of trial. (Id. at 11-12, ¶¶ 21-22.) The limited testing given to petitioner by Dr. Maloney was not equivalent to “a comprehensive neuropsychological evaluation under the then-prevailing professional standards.” (Id. at 12, ¶ 22.) According to Dr. Gur, petitioner’s brain damage existed at the time of the crimes and prevented petitioner from understanding or responding appropriately to his victims’ expressions of resistance and fear. His inability to perceive emotion accurately makes it difficult for petitioner to judge things correctly, especially in an emotionally volatile situation. (Id. at 15, ¶ 29.) Moreover, Dr. Gur concluded that petitioner’s brain damage caused petitioner to be in a dissociative state when he committed the crimes, preventing him from forming the intent to commit murder and rape. Petitioner’s dissociation would have helped explain petitioner’s detailed confession, his inability to recall the details of the crimes during his testimony and his persistent inability to explain why he committed the crimes. (Id. at 17, ¶ 35.) Specifically, evidence of dissociation would have allowed counsel to argue that petitioner did not actually remember the details of the crime, but, instead, that the police fed him much of the information following his arrest. Petitioner subsequently confessed in great detail. Despite his confession, petitioner could not in fact remember many things about the crime because he was in a dissociative state. Accordingly, counsel could argue that petitioner was testifying truthfully at the penalty phase when he stated that he could not recall many things about the crimes.
Dr. Lewis also testified that petitioner’s capacity to form the specific intent to rape and kill was substantially impaired on the night of the crimes due to “a constellation of neuropsychiatric vulnerabilities ... and extreme intra-family stressors ... which engendered his extreme[,] uncontrollable[,] violent acts. The knowledge of these biopsychosocial vulnerabilities and the appreciation of their role in [the] offenses are vital to understanding his compromised mental functioning on the nights of the murder[s].” (Lewis 8/13/03 Deck at 37, ¶ 86.)
This evidence certainly could have helped at trial. Evidence that petitioner could not in fact form the specific intent to murder or rape would have provided a more solid defense than one relying only on petitioner’s intoxication alone. While petitioner has put forth evidence that could have caused at least one juror to vote differently, petitioner has not demonstrated by a reasonable probability that this new evidence would have been successful at the guilt phase. Even if petitioner used experts to explain why the confession was detailed but petitioner later could not recall much at trial, a reasonable jury likely would have rejected a diminished capacity defense. The very similar factual circumstances of the crimes, taking place just a week apart, could support a conclusion that petitioner was able to premeditate, deliberate or plan, all of which undercut a diminished capacity defense. Finally, within a year and a half of trial, a voter referendum changed the law to eliminate the dense of diminished capacity due to mental disease, defect or mental disorder. See CabPenal Code § 28. That context may have lessened the likelihood of success of a diminished capacity defense at the guilt phase.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claims 5(C)(7) and 5(D)(32) to the extent they raise guilt phase claims but will consider counsel’s deficiencies in the cumulative error analysis.
c. Penalty phase prejudice
In evaluating prejudice at the penalty phase, the Court must weigh the evidence in aggravation against the evidence in mitigation, including the evidence counsel could have presented but did not. Petitioner must show a reasonable probability of a different verdict. Williams (Terry), 529 U.S. at 398, 120 S.Ct. 1495.
“More than any other singular factor, mental defects have been respected as a reason for leniency in our criminal justice system.” Caro, 280 F.3d at 1258. While counsel presented some evidence of petitioner’s emotional problems, that testimony did not come close to painting an accurate picture of petitioner’s neurological deficits. Dr. Gur testified that petitioner’s brain damage has greatly impacted the way petitioner both perceives the world and functions in relationships. Petitioner harbors a skewed picture of reality. He suffers from a profound impairment in his ability to perceive emotions accurately, often mistaking happiness and sadness for anger and fear. Petitioner has suffered from this problem as early as preschool, and it is heightened when he is experiencing extreme emotion or stress. Petitioner attempts to cope with his inability to perceive emotions accurately by using other cues, but this coping mechanism is quite limited due to the mental illness of petitioner’s adoptive parents. (Gur 2/8/05 Decl. at 12, ¶¶ 23-24.)
Moreover, petitioner’s brain damage also may explain his inability to recall the details of the crime when questioned by the police. Petitioner testified in his deposition that the police spent several hours discussing the crime and showing him pictures before recording his detailed statement. However, petitioner’s penalty phase testimony was replete with assertions that petitioner could not remember what happened. (Id. at 15-16, ¶¶ 30-32.) The clinical data suggest that petitioner actually cannot recall significant parts of the crime, not that he is being evasive or feigning forgetfulness. (Id. at 16, ¶31.) Dr. Clausen also opined that petitioner dissociated as a coping mechanism and that he dissociated during the crimes. (Clausen Deck at 188-124, ¶¶ 284-89, 291-92, 294-96; see also Lewis 8/15/03 Deck at 33-34, ¶ 82 (opining that the circumstances of Ryan’s murder suggest that petitioner was in a dissociative state).)
Counsel’s failure to investigate and present petitioner’s deeply troubled home life was also quite damaging. As part of the evidentiary hearing, several of petitioner’s experts have created social histories. Dr. Clausen testified that petitioner has experienced recurring trauma, from his conception through the crime. This trauma included having a neglectful primary caretaker afflicted with schizophrenia and a paranoid, oft-absent father. She discussed, at length, the impact of petitioner’s schizophrenic mother on petitioner. Dr. Lewis diagnosed petitioner with bipolar mood disorder. (Lewis 8/15/03 Decl. at 36, ¶ 84.)
Counsel’s failure to investigate petitioner’s background and medical history and to have a neurological exam conducted resulted in a haphazard, incoherent mitigation case. Counsel failed to put on evidence that would have served counsel’s stated goal in his penalty phase opening argument: to “indicate how the defendant go to be where the defendant is” and “the type of individual that the defendant was at the time of the commission of the offenses and potentially the type of individual he is today.” (13 RT 34326.)
Counsel could have presented evidence, however, that would have explained how petitioner became who he was at the time of the crimes. Dr. Gur explained the effect of petitioner’s brain damage this way:
I would think that he started off with a bad brain because of his biological background. Growing up as a fetus in a 14-year-old girl who abuses alcohol during pregnancy, I mean, we already know that children born to 14 year olds and 15 year olds or even 16 year olds have significant deficits as a group. So some brain damage comes just right from there.
Mother consuming alcohol, the more we know about the effects of alcohol during pregnancy, the more we find that they are much more devastating than we ever thought .... Then you have the forceps delivery, and then you have evidence of child neglect, abuse, physical punishments, inept parenting by the adoptive parents.
You then have a series of head injuries from motorcycle crashes and other forms of head injuries. You add to that early substance abuse, which often happens with those kids. They know there is something screwed up about their brains, so a lot of times they medicate themselves. Street drugs make them feel better in the best case or give them a rational explanation for their abnormal thought processes in the worst case. And they, themselves, as I mentioned, they go straight to the frontal lobe and retard the development of the frontal lobe.
You put all of that together, and the mystery becomes how he managed to reach 18 without committing serious crimes before that?
(Gur Depo. at 461.) Dr. Gur also touched on the impact of petitioner’s home life: “At best, I understand, his mother was ineffective and withdrawn and eventually left the home. And his father was prone to outbursts of anger and rage and was abusive psychologically and physically. So you put that factor in with someone who has the vulnerability that Francis had growing up, and it’s a prescription for disaster. It’s a prescription for someone who will really never develop normally and will not have much of a chance to develop the mental and intellectual and personal capacity to cope with life’s stresses.” (Gur Depo. at 447; see also Lewis 8/15/03 Decl. at 31, ¶ 77 (testifying that “[i]t is hard to imagine how a more genetically resilient child could have weathered the family environment and adapted appropriately to society, much less a child with Francis’s inherent vulnerabilities to mental illness.”); 2 Lewis Depo. at 382 (testifying that a vulnerable child raised in a psychotic environment with incredible stress creates an aberrant human being who cannot function as others do).)
In addition, counsel completely missed an opportunity to tell a narrative that could have evoked mercy. As Dr. Clausen testified:
The story of Francis Hernandez is a harrowing tale of genetic and environmental risk, extensive child abuse and profound child neglect, chronic traumatization by a severely mentally ill primary caretaker in the context of a markedly impaired co-parent unable and/or unwilling to provide minimal protection, and a baffling series of missed opportunities for intervention and tertiary prevention by family members and mental health, education, and juvenile justice professionals. As was feared by and foreshadowed with the comments of his closest family members, Francis lived a childhood that was literally a recipe for disaster and tragedy. His genetic and adoptive families, together with the community in which he lived, produced an adolescent entirely unequipped to understand his social world, negotiate interpersonal relationships, appropriately manage strong emotions, and seek help for himself. He was rejected in útero, abandoned at birth, neglected during infancy, abused throughout childhood, and ignored during adolescence. It is impossible to place oneself in the shoes of Francis Hernandez at the time of the capital crimes for which he has been convicted simply because the rest of us cannot possibly imagine the daily hell in which he spent his life.
(Clausen Decl. at 127, ¶ 305; see also Clausen Depo. at 119 (“I see more the psychotic mothering that he received growing up as the most central factor in developing who he was as an individual, and resulting in who he was and what sorts of coping strategies he had, and the resources he didn’t have; that the physical and sexual abuse and the role that his father played and the role that his genetic history played were all important and significant, but the most central feature about what was going on was the psychotic mother.”).)
Counsel’s failure to conduct an adequate investigation into petitioner’s background, his medical history and his neurological impairments had serious consequences at the penalty phase. Counsel’s failures deprived petitioner of the effective assistance of counsel. See, e.g., Rompilla, 545 U.S. at 390-93, 125 S.Ct. 2456 (granting petition where counsel failed to uncover evidence that petitioner suffered from organic brain damage, that petitioner often had no a caretaker as a teenager and that the house was filthy); Williams (Terry), 529 U.S. at 395-99, 120 S.Ct. 1495 (finding counsel ineffective upon failing to investigate and present evidence of petitioner’s nightmarish childhood); Ainsworth, 268 F.3d at 874-78 (granting relief where counsel failed to investigate and present evidence of petitioner’s troubled background and emotional instability); Wallace v. Stewart, 184 F.3d 1112, 1115-16 (9th Cir.1999) (granting petition where counsel conducted no factual investigation into petitioner’s dysfunctional background). Petitioner has demonstrated prejudice sufficient to undermine confidence in the penalty verdict, by showing a likelihood of a different verdict at the penalty phase.
Accordingly, the Court GRANTS Claims 5(C)(7) and 5(D)(32) as to the penalty phase and will also consider counsel’s deficiencies in the cumulative error analysis.
7. Counsel’s failure to challenge petitioner’s competence to confess, testify and stand trial (Claim 5(D)(26))
In Claim 5(D)(26), petitioner contends that trial counsel failed to investigate the gravity of petitioner’s mental, emotional and psychological impairment before, during and after the crime, as well as during his confinement in county jail. As a result, trial counsel failed to challenge petitioner’s competence to give a statement to the police, to testify and to stand trial.
“It has long been accepted that a person whose mental condition is such that he lacks the capacity to understand the nature and object of the proceedings against him, to consult with counsel, and to assist in preparing his defense may not be subjected to a trial.” Drope v. Missouri, 420 U.S. 162, 171, 95 S.Ct. 896, 43 L.Ed.2d 103 (1975). The test for competence to stand trial is whether the defendant “has sufficient present ability to consult with his lawyer with a reasonable degree of rational understanding-and whether he has a rational as well as factual understanding of the proceedings against him.” Dusky v. United States, 362 U.S. 402, 402, 80 S.Ct. 788, 4 L.Ed.2d 824 (1960) (per curiam) (internal quotation marks omitted). “[C]ompetence to stand trial does not consist merely of passively observing the proceedings. Rather, it requires the mental acuity to see, hear and digest the evidence, and the ability to communicate with counsel in helping prepare an effective defense.” Odle v. Woodford, 238 F.3d 1084, 1089 (9th Cir.2001). “Although no particular facts signal incompetence, suggestive evidence includes a defendant’s demeanor before the trial court, previous irrational behavior, and available medical evaluations.” Moran v. Godinez, 57 F.3d 690, 695 (9th Cir.1994).
“[Although retrospective competency hearings are disfavored, they are permissible whenever a court can conduct a meaningful hearing to evaluate retrospectively the competency of the defendant.” Id. at 696 (internal citations omitted). “[M]edical reports contemporaneous to the time of the initial hearing greatly increase the chance for an accurate retrospective evaluation of a defendant’s competence.” Id.; see also Deere v. Woodford, 339 F.3d 1084, 1086-87 (9th Cir.2003) (pointing to a doctor’s examination of petitioner within several days of guilty plea as particularly probative of his mental status at trial).
Petitioner has submitted a great deal of evidence concerning his mental state as part of the evidentiary hearing. This evidence includes testimony that petitioner suffers from bipolar mood disorder and organic brain damage. (Lewis 8/15/03 Decl. at 34-36, ¶ 84; Gur 2/8/05 Deck at 10, ¶ 18.) Brain damage impairs petitioner’s ability to organize and recall information, causes petitioner to misperceive emotions and makes it difficult for petitioner to modulate his emotional response appropriately. (Gur 2/8/05 Deck at 10-11, ¶¶ 18-20.) This evidence does not suggest, however, that petitioner lacked the capacity to understand that he was being tried for capital murder and faced the death penalty. While many of petitioner’s experts have testified that he lacked the capacity to form the requisite intents at the time of the crime, none has testified that he was incompetent to stand trial. (Lewis 8/15/03 Deck at 37, ¶ 87; 6/8/04 Deck at 15-17, ¶¶ 30-32, 35.) Moreover, none of the experts who evaluated petitioner at the time of trial found him incompetent. Petitioner has not met his burden of establishing by a preponderance of the evidence that he was incompetent to stand trial.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(26).
8. Counsel’s decision to put petitioner on the stand at the penalty phase (Claim 5(D)(27))
In Claim 5(D)(27), petitioner contends that trial counsel made a misguided, inappropriate and ill-considered decision to put petitioner on the stand during the penalty phase. Petitioner also argues that his mental condition caused him to appear unsympathetic and cold, which alienated the jury. Furthermore, trial counsel exacerbated the prejudice to petitioner by failing to investigate and present mental health evidence that would have explained petitioner’s affect.
Counsel moved for separate juries for the guilt and penalty phases because he wanted petitioner to testify at the guilt phase. Counsel thought that petitioner would need to testify at the guilt phase to establish the amount of alcohol he consumed before the crimes. However, counsel did not want petitioner to testify at the penalty phase due to his appearance, his ability to testify and the content of his testimony. (10 CDD 40^11.) The trial court denied the motion.
Inexplicably, counsel put petitioner on the stand at the penalty phase. Counsel wanted petitioner to testify about his background and home life, but counsel asked only about the facts of the crime. (1 CT 289; 10 CDD 40-43; 13 RT 3398-3415.) On cross examination, the prosecutor questioned petitioner extensively about the facts of the crime. (13 RT 3416-65; 14 RT 3486-3551.) Petitioner testified countless times that he could not remember many of the gruesome details of the crimes to which he had confessed, making him seem cold, callous and uncaring. (See generally, 13 RT 3416-65; 14 RT 3486-3551.) He also testified that neither victim was crying or upset during the crimes. (13 RT 3432-33; 14 RT 3534-3.) Petitioner’s testimony, including lengthy cross examination, consumed much more time than any other penalty phase witness.
Counsel performed incompetently. First, counsel put petitioner on the stand at the penalty phase, despite his earlier decision that petitioner would not make a good penalty phase witness. This is not a case where counsel “could not have predicted just how damaging placing [petitioner] on the stand would be.” Allen v. Woodford, 395 F.3d 979, 1000 (9th Cir.2005). No rational, reasoned basis supported this decision. Moreover, counsel questioned petitioner only about the facts and circumstances of the crime, rather than about petitioner’s personal background. See Harris v. Wood, 64 F.3d 1432, 1437 (9th Cir.1995) (finding deficient counsel’s failure to question defendant in accordance with counsel’s purpose in calling defendant to testify). Even worse, petitioner’s testimony that he could not remember many details of the crime made petitioner seem unremorseful and cruel. Petitioner’s testimony that the victims were not crying or upset during the crime were statements that “a reasonable jury could have easily chosen to disbelieve” as “self-serving uncorroborated testimony.” United States v. Nicholson, 677 F.2d 706, 709 (9th Cir.1982); see also United States v. Cisneros, 448 F.2d 298 (9th Cir.1971) (“A trier of fact is not compelled to accept and believe the self-serving stories of vitally interested defendants. Their evidence may not only be disbelieved, but from the totality of the circumstances, including the manner in which they testify, a contrary conclusion may be properly drawn.”) Counsel’s performance fell below an objective standard of reasonableness.
Petitioner’s penalty phase testimony, which focused exclusively on the circumstances of the crime, caused petitioner unmitigated prejudice. Petitioner’s testimony consumed the bulk of the evidence presented at the penalty phase. As counsel predicted before trial, petitioner’s ability to testify and the content of his testimony did nothing to mitigate the crime. Petitioner appeared callous and dishonest, and the prosecutor argued those points. (14 RT 3636-64 (“[H]e was not telling the truth. Either — when he says[,] [‘]I don’t remember,[’] he is either such a callous individual that he really doesn’t remember — what would a normal person remember concerning what happened in this case? A normal person would probably think about it constantly, have nightmares about it, and relive it every moment of the day. Is that the type of individual we have ... or a person who isn’t and won’t admit what he did and who lies about it when he say[s], T don’t remember. After all it was 20, 25 months ago[.]”))
Moreover, petitioner’s testimony did not make him seem less culpable. In fact, his repeated assertions that he could not recall the facts of the crime and his testimony that the victims did not cry or seem upset made him seem markedly more culpable than less. Counsel also failed to take other steps that would have ameliorated the harm caused by petitioner’s testimony, and those errors exacerbated the situation. For example, counsel could have had Dr. Maloney testify about petitioner’s psychological problems, and that testimony might have explained petitioner’s odd affect while testifying. (JTD at P00829-30; see also Discussion of Claim 5(C)(1), 5(C)(2), supra.) Moreover, if counsel had requested a neurological examination of petitioner, an expert could have testified about petitioner’s inability to accurately perceive emotions and to modulate his emotional response appropriately. (Gur 2/8/05 Deck at 10-11, ¶¶ 18-20; see also Discussion of Claim 5(C)(7), supra.) Such testimony would have helped to explain petitioner’s testimony that the victims were not upset or that he did not know they were upset. Finally, if counsel had conducted an adequate investigation into petitioner’s background, he could have put other witnesses on the stand to discuss petitioner’s background. Many lay witnesses were able and willing to discuss petitioner’s life, other than those who offered thin pleas for mercy at the penalty phase. (See, e.g., declarations of Barbara Graziano (maternal aunt), Joy Turner (preschool teacher), Judy Williams (ex-girlfriend’s mother), Moms Silverstein (childhood friend); see also Discussion of Claims 5(B)(3)(C) and 5(C)(3), supra.) An adequate investigation also would have revealed evidence that petitioner had a history of dissociation. Expert testimony that he was in a dissociative state at the time of the crimes would have explained petitioner’s inability to recall the details of the crimes at the penalty phase. (Clausen Deck at 118-24, ¶¶284-89, 291-92, 294-96; Lewis 8/15/03 Deck at 27-28, ¶¶ 68-70 & 36-37, ¶ 85-87; Gur 6/8/04 Deck at 17, ¶ 35; see also Discussion of Claims 5(C)(7), 5(D)(32), supra.)
Counsel’s decision to put petitioner on the stand, without any psychological evidence to explain petitioner’s demeanor, was utterly devastating.
Accordingly, the Court GRANTS Claim 5(D)(27).
C. Other IAC Claims
1. Counsel’s failure to challenge the State’s theory that Kathy Ryan was kidnapped (Claim 5(D)(2))
In Claim 5(D)(2), petitioner alleges that trial counsel failed to challenge the State’s contention that Kathy Ryan was kidnapped from her home on the evening of her murder, as evidence tending to show that Ryan willingly met petitioner on the night of the murder would have supported petitioner’s claim that Ryan consented to having sex with him.
Again, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner cannot show deficiency or prejudice. Kathy Ryan’s stepmother, Barbara Ryan, offered the following testimony at trial: At about 5:30 the morning Kathy’s body was discovered, Barbara awoke to find the living room lights on and the front sliding glass doors and screens open. She went to Kathy’s bedroom and found that Kathy’s lights were on, Kathy’s bed was still made and Kathy’s bedroom window was open with no screen in it. A few hours later, Barbara discovered Kathy’s pool cue and jacket on the living room floor near the open sliding glass door. On her way to her car, Barbara found Kathy’s purse outside by the pool with the contents spilled out on the ground. (8 RT 1831-35.)
Given this evidence, petitioner can show neither deficiency nor prejudice. Counsel could have concluded that it would have been futile, and potentially damaging to counsel’s credibility, to attempt to argue that Kathy Ryan left willingly with petitioner. See Rupe v. Wood, 93 F.3d 1434, 1440 (9th Cir.1996) (holding that failure to take futile action can not be ineffective). Moreover, even if counsel did attempt to challenge the kidnapping allegation, it would be quite attenuated for counsel to argue successfully that because Kathy Ryan may have met petitioner willingly on the night of the murder, she also consented to having sex with him. The physical evidence strongly suggested that Kathy Ryan was raped prior to hear death and that she likely was penetrated forcefully by an object. (9 RT 2002, 2017-20, 2029-30.) Petitioner cannot prevail on Claim 5(D)(2).
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(2).
2. Counsel’s failure to put on evidence about petitioner’s level of inebriation on the nights of both crimes (Claim 5(D)(3))
In claim 5(D)(3), petitioner argues that trial counsel failed to elicit corroborating evidence from eye witnesses that petitioner consumed large quantities of alcohol and drugs on the nights of both crimes.
As with the other claims of IAC, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that counsel’s inadequate representation prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Trial counsel put Christopher Esparza on the stand. Esparza spent the afternoon preceding Kathy Ryan’s murder with petitioner. (12 RT 3023-26.) Esparza testified that while he and petitioner were together, they stopped at a liquor store and purchased two six-packs of beer. (12 RT 3024.) Esparza saw petitioner drink at least four beers during their last hour together. (12 RT 3025-26.) In addition, trial counsel presented the testimony of Dr. Amer Rayyes, who testified that petitioner is an alcoholic and that every time petitioner drank, he would drink until he was intoxicated. (12 RT 3058.) Petitioner’s confession stated that he was drunk on the night of Edna Bristol’s murder. (Confession Transcript at 3.)
Petitioner cannot prevail by alleging that counsel failed to put on cumulative evidence. Babbitt v. Calderon, 151 F.3d 1170, 1174 (9th Cir.1998) (holding that “it was not unreasonable for counsel not to pursue such testimony when it was largely cumulative of the testimony” already offered). Moreover, petitioner cannot show prejudice. The jury heard evidence that petitioner had been drinking heavily preceding both murders and heard expert testimony about the effect alcohol had on him. Petitioner has shown neither deficiency nor prejudice.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(3).
3. Counsel’s failure to investigate the audio-taped confession for tampering (Claim 5(D)(4))
In Claim 5(D)(4), petitioner contends that trial counsel failed to conduct an adequate investigation of the audio-taped confession for any possible tampering with the tape. Trial counsel also failed to have petitioner listen to the tape and provide his comments on it, or to question petitioner about the circumstances surrounding the confession.
Again, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that counsel’s deficiency prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner has not put forth any allegations or evidence to show what counsel would have learned if he had conducted an independent examination of the audio-taped confession for possible tampering. He also has not provided any evidence of what petitioner would have told counsel if petitioner had listened to and commented on the confession. Petitioner’s “claim of [deficiency and] prejudice amounts to mere speculation.” Cooks v. Spalding, 660 F.2d 738, 740 (9th Cir.1981); see also James v. Borg, 24 F.3d at 26.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(4).
4. Counsel’s failure to challenge probable cause to arrest petitioner and search his van (Claim 5(D)(5))
In Claim 5(D)(5), petitioner alleges that trial counsel failed to rebut the prosecution’s contention that the arresting officers had sufficient probable cause to arrest petitioner and to search his van.
As stated, petitioner must show that counsel’s performance fell below the standard of care and that this deficiency prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527. “Where defense counsel’s failure to litigate a Fourth Amendment claim competently is the principal allegation of ineffectiveness, the defendant must also prove that his Fourth Amendment claim is meritorious and that there is a reasonable probability that the verdict would have been different absent the excludable evidence in order to demonstrate actual prejudice.” Kimmelman v. Morrison, 477 U.S. 365, 375, 106 S.Ct. 2574, 91 L.Ed.2d 305 (1986).
Petitioner argues that “[a] significant aspect of probable cause to search [his] van was the alleged observation of a carpet that was similar to fibers found in one of the victim’s pubic hairs.” (Petr’s 8/23/07 Brief at 21). Petitioner also contends that “[t]he police claimed to have seen a ‘rust-brown’ carpet on the floor of Mr. Hernandez’s van.” (Id.) In support of these contentions, petitioner points to the prosecutor’s opening argument, which noted that the coroner found burnt orange and rust-colored carpet fibers, the same as the carpet found in petitioner’s van, on victim Edna Bristol. (8 RT 1750.) He also cites the statements of police officer Ronald Nelson. Officer Nelson testified that carpet was pulled over the backseat of petitioner’s van, but that he could still see part of the carpet, which appeared to be “red” or “rust-colored” and that he saw “several rubber bands, green and brown, on the shifting column of the vehicle.” (1 RT 68A-69A.)
Petitioner alleges that the arresting officer could not have observed the carpet in the back of petitioner’s van and that counsel performed deficiently in both failing to discover that impossibility and arguing that there was no probable cause to search the van. For example, police officer Mac Lyman testified at trial that “[y]ou could only see the driver’s compartment of the van. There was a blanket covering the rear portion of the van.” (11 RT 2858, 2861.) Another witness, Robert Beeler, testified that he thought the windows on petitioner’s van had been painted. (9 RT 2196.)
At the motion to suppress the confession, however, the prosecution offered the testimony of Long Beach police officer Paul Chastain, who located and looked inside petitioner’s van before it was searched. (1 RT 213A-214A.) Officer Chastain testified that he saw orange carpet inside the van. (1 RT 214A.) While he testified that he could not see anything in the rear of the van (1 RT 221A-222A), he did see orange-colored carpet “between the seats,” and “on the driver’s side,” “on the floor.” (1 RT 225A.) Given this testimony, it is difficult for petitioner to establish that counsel performed deficiently by failing to argue that the police could not see the color of the carpet from outside the van.
Even if counsel performed deficiently, petitioner cannot show prejudice. As stated in Strickland, “a court need not determine whether counsel’s performance was deficient before examining the prejudice suffered by the defendant as a result of the alleged deficiencies .... If it is easier to dispose of an ineffectiveness claim on the ground of lack of sufficient prejudice, which we expect will often be so, that course should be followed.” Strickland, 466 U.S. at 697, 104 S.Ct. 2052. Petitioner “must establish that had” counsel challenged the visibility of the carpet fibers from outside the van, then “there was a reasonable probability that the evidence would have been suppressed” and petitioner would have enjoyed a more favorable verdict. Lowry v. Lewis, 21 F.3d 344, 346-47 (9th Cir.1994) (citing Morrison, 477 U.S. at 375, 106 S.Ct. 2574). First, the conflicting testimony about the visibility of the carpet from outside the van might have caused counsel to raise different arguments at the suppression hearing, but those arguments would not necessarily have negated a finding of probable cause. Cf. Gerstein v. Pugh, 420 U.S. 103, 121, 95 S.Ct. 854, 43 L.Ed.2d 54 (1975) (“[A probable cause determination] does not require the fine resolution of conflicting evidence that a reasonable-doubt or even a preponderance standard demands, and credibility determinations are seldom crucial in deciding whether the evidence supports a reasonable belief in guilt.”) In addition, there likely was probable cause to search petitioner’s van even without the orange-colored carpet fibers. The police found “[tjwenty-three small turquoise rubber bands ... on the ground near [Edna Bristol’s] body.” Hernandez, 47 Cal.3d at 328, 253 Cal.Rptr. 199, 763 P.2d 1289. Before arresting petitioner and searching his van, the police looked inside the van from the outside and saw “several greenish-turquoise rubberbands on the driver shifting column.” (11 RT 2859; see also 1 RT 69A (“[T]here was [sic] also several rubber bands, green and brown, on the shifting column of the vehicle.”).) The rubber-bands, on their own, probably supported a finding of probable cause to search petitioner’s van, given the other facts and circumstances suggesting that the same perpetrator committed the two murders, placing petitioner with Kathy Ryn on the night of her murder and connecting petitioner to Edna Bristol about a month before Bristol’s murder. See, e.g., United States v. Diaz, 491 F.3d 1074, 1078 (9th Cir.2007) (“[P]robable cause means a fair probability that contraband or evidence of a crime will be found in a particular place, based on the totality of the circumstances.”) (internal quotation marks and citations omitted); see also Maryland v. Pringle, 540 U.S. 366, 370-71, 124 S.Ct. 795, 157 L.Ed.2d 769 (2003) (“The probable-cause standard is a practical, nontechnical conception that deals with the factual and practical considerations of everyday life on which reasonable and prudent men, not legal technicians, act .... The probable-cause standard is incapable of precise definition or quantification into percentages because it deals with probabilities and depends on the totality of the circumstances.”) (internal quotation marks and citations and omitted); Texas v. Brown, 460 U.S. 730, 742, 103 S.Ct. 1535, 75 L.Ed.2d 502 (1983) (“[PJrobable cause is a flexible, common-sense standard .... A practical, nontechnical probability that incriminating evidence is involved is all that is required.”) (internal quotation marks and citation omitted). Petitioner has failed to show a reasonable probability that the evidence recovered from the van would have been suppressed, and, consequently, that petitioner would have been acquitted on any charges, had trial counsel challenged whether the police could see the color of the carpet fibers from outside petitioner’s van.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(5).
5. Counsel’s failure to investigate and present evidence of potential Miranda violations (Claim 5(D)(6))
It is difficult to understand exactly what petitioner alleges in Claim 5(D)(6). Petitioner seems to contend that trial counsel failed to investigate and present available evidence to support petitioner’s assertion that the police delayed advising petitioner of his rights and that the police refused to honor petitioner’s request for an attorney. This evidence allegedly consists of police reports concerning petitioner’s interrogation in a separate pair of murders in San Luis Obispo County. The police reports purportedly state that petitioner was not explicitly advised of his rights and that the police ignored petitioner’s request for counsel during petitioner’s interrogation for the murders in San Luis Obispo County. (The parties have filed a joint stipulation that petitioner was never charged with those crimes.) Petitioner alleges that the reports show that the Long Beach police and the San Luis Obispo County police, in separate interrogations, similarly mistreated petitioner by delaying the advisement of his rights and by not honoring his request for counsel promptly. Petitioner contends that this acknowledgment “is highly probative of whether Mr. Hernandez was denied his right to counsel at the time he was interrogated by the Long Beach police.” (Petr’s 8/24/08 Brief at 24.) Petitioner suggests that if counsel had discovered and presented this evidence during the suppression motion, petitioner’s confession would have been suppressed and he would have been acquitted.
As stated, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
This claim fails. Petitioner has not cited to the alleged police reports in the petition or his merits briefing, and a thorough search of the records and briefs did not uncover any such reports. Because the alleged police reports are unavailable for the Court’s inspection, the Court cannot evaluate the alleged factual underpinnings of this claim. Even if such reports exist, it is difficult to imagine that they contain an admission that the police violated petitioner’s rights. (See, e.g., 2 RT 343A-346A, 348A-349A, 2 RT 493A-95A (detailing testimony by police officers that they read petitioner his rights about ten minutes after they apprehended him and that petitioner did not request an attorney). Moreover, even if the Long Beach police officers advised their San Luis Obispo County counterparts that petitioner “behaved similarly” when he was arrested for the Long Beach murders, that fact by itself does not suggest that the Long Beach police violated petitioner’s rights when they interrogated him about the murders of Kathy Ryn and Edna Bristol. It is just as likely that petitioner acted similarly in the two interrogations because he was being questioned about a double murder that could lead to capital charges, as it is to presume that his behavior was related to the police failing to Mirandize him and or to provide counsel. Petitioner’s claim of deficiency is unsupported by the record and fails as both conclusory and speculative. Jones, 66 F.3d at 204-05 (“It is well-settled that ‘conclusory allegations which are not supported by a statement of specific facts do not warrant habeas relief.’ ”) (quoting James v. Borg, 24 F.3d 20, 26 (9th Cir.1994)); cf. Allison, 431 U.S. at 75 n. 7, 97 S.Ct. 1621 (holding that the petition must allege facts that demonstrate the possibility of constitutional error).
Similarly, petitioner cannot show prejudice. Even assuming that counsel performed deficiently, petitioner would have to show that (1) absent counsel’s failure to discover the police reports, which were purportedly withheld, and (2) excepting counsel’s consequent failure to offer the police reports in support of the motion to suppress, the trial court would have suppressed the confession, and petitioner would have been acquitted. That possibility is too attenuated and speculative to support a finding of prejudice, particularly when the trial court held an extensive hearing on the legality of petitioner’s confession. Cooks, 660 F.2d at 740 (holding that petitioner’s claim of prejudice amounted to “mere speculation”).
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(6).
6. Counsel’s failure to argue to that petitioner’s arraignment was unreasonably delayed in support of the motion to suppress (Claim 5(D)(8))
In Claim 5(D)(8), petitioner alleges that trial counsel failed to move to suppress petitioner’s February 6, 1981 confession on the grounds that his arraignment had been unreasonably delayed.
The Court laid out the relevant facts in its summary judgment order:
Petitioner was arrested at his residence at approximately 2:10 p.m. on February 4, 1981. He was advised of his Miranda rights. The advisement and petitioner’s waiver were recorded on audiotape. (1 RT 307A.) The police interviewed petitioner until 8:21 p.m. that evening. During the interview, petitioner made many incriminating statements. He then agreed to provide blood, saliva and hair samples. Petitioner was taken to a hospital for that purpose. In the early morning hours of February 6, 1981, he was again interviewed by police. Before the interview, petitioner was reminded of his constitutional rights and asked if he remembered those rights. (1 RT 313A.) He agreed to provide a dental impression and was taken to the hospital for that purpose.
(Summary Judgment Order at 24.)
“[T]he Fourth Amendment requires a prompt judicial determination of probable cause as a prerequisite to an extended pretrial detention following a warrantless arrest.” County of Riverside v. McLaughlin, 500 U.S. 44, 47, 111 S.Ct. 1661, 114 L.Ed.2d 49 (1991) (citing Gerstein v. Pugh, 420 U.S. 103, 124, 95 S.Ct. 854, 43 L.Ed.2d 54 (1975)). Specifically, “a jurisdiction that provides judicial determinations of probable cause within 48 hours of arrest will, as a general matter, comply with the promptness requirement.” McLaughlin, 500 U.S. at 56, 111 S.Ct. 1661; but see McLaughlin, 500 U.S. at 56-57, 111 S.Ct. 1661 (explaining that the 48-hour rule will generally provide immunity from systematic challenges, but that it will not bar a claim where the individual can prove that despite providing a probable cause determination within 48 hours, that determination was still delayed unreasonably).
Petitioner argues that he was not arraigned within 48 hours of his arrest, as required by McLaughlin and California Penal Code Section 825. In fact, petitioner contends that he was not arraigned until March 4, 1981, a full month after his arrest. (Petr’s 8/23/08 Brief at 26.) Petitioner therefore alleges that trial counsel was ineffective for failing to argue that the unreasonable delay in his arraignment was only for the purpose of investigation and that, therefore, petitioner’s February 6, 1981 confession should have been suppressed because it was the fruit of the poisonous tree. (Petr’s 8/23/08 Brief at 27.)
Petitioner must demonstrate that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner cannot prevail on this claim. Petitioner cannot demonstrate that counsel was deficient for failing to argue that the February 6, 1981 confession should have been suppressed based on an unreasonable delay in arraignment. The facts show that petitioner was arrested at about 2:10 p.m. on February 4, 1981. He began to give the police a tape-recorded confession that same day at 7:38 p.m. Petitioner then offered another confession in the early morning hours of February 6, 1981. Both of these confessions fall within the 48-hour rule, as the time for a probable cause determination would not have expired until 2:10 p.m. on February 6, 1981. In other words, neither confession was made “past the time that the delay was unreasonable.” Cf. Anderson v. Calderon, 232 F.3d 1053, 1071 (9th Cir.2000). Therefore, counsel was not deficient for failing to argue at the suppression hearing that the February 6, 1981 confession should have been suppressed because there was an unreasonable delay in petitioner’s arraignment.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(8).
7. Counsel’s failure to challenge State’s theory that petitioner used a baseball bat to assault victims (Claim 5(D)(10))
In Claim 5(D)(10), petitioner argues that trial counsel failed to challenge the State’s contention that petitioner sexually assaulted the victims with a baseball bat.
At trial, the State presented the testimony of Joan Dr. Shipley, a pathologist who worked for the Los Angeles County medical examiner. (9 RT 1968-89.) When asked whether the condition of the victims’ bodies was consistent with or excluded penetration by a penis, Dr. Shipley testified that the damage to the anus and vagina of both victims indicated “a very severe degree of trauma, and the wounds in both girls [were] extremely similar and extremely rare.” (9 RT 2030.) She also described the injuries as “very extensive,” and stated that “in ten years’ experience, it’s almost been unique to find it in these two cases, and it certainly suggests a very large object and repetitive injuries.” (Id.) When asked, Dr. Shipley opined that the wounds could be consistent with a baseball bat. (Id.)
The prosecution also called Barbara Johnson, a forensic serologist for the Los Angeles County Sheriff’s Department. (10 RT 2823-33.) Johnson testified that she found human blood on the baseball bat retrieved from petitioner’s van. (Id. at 2394.) The blood “was on the side of the bat and about the middle portion of the bat.” (Id. at 2491.) She checked the rest of the bat for blood but did not find any. (Id. at 2492.) She also examined a “white crusty stain at the end of the bat” for semen but did not detect any. (Id.) Finally, Johnson testified that the blood on the baseball bat was likely petitioner’s blood. (Id. at 2496.)
Again, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner’s claim cannot prevail. Even if trial counsel’s failure to challenge the prosecutor’s intimation that petitioner used a baseball bat to assault both victims was deficient, petitioner cannot show prejudice. Any lay person could have concluded based on the evidence presented that the baseball bat was not used to assault the victims. If petitioner used the baseball bat to assault the victims, one would expect the bat to contain more than a small amount of blood, and that blood would not be only in the center of the bat but would extend from either end of the bat. Moreover, the blood found in the center of the baseball bat was petitioner’s blood rather than the blood of one of the victim’s. A small amount of the petitioner’s blood in the center of the baseball bat is not consistent with the bat being used to sodomize or otherwise assault the victims.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(10).
8. Counsel’s failure to obtain all discovery (Claim 5(D)(11))
In Claim 5(D)(11), petitioner contends that trial counsel failed to move to discover or obtain a complete record of all the material in the possession of the State, particularly all of the notes and reports made by the police and the coroner, among others. Trial counsel also failed to obtain clinical notes and records of the scientific tests performed by the State, as well as the conditions in which the physical evidence was maintained prior to testing.
As with other IAC claims, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner cannot prevail on this claim because he cannot show prejudice. The physical evidence helped to establish petitioner’s identity as the assailant, which was not an issue at trial. Petitioner confessed to the murders, and that confession was admitted. Petitioner’s defense at trial was not mistaken identity but that he accidentally killed the victims, that he suffered from diminished capacity or both. To demonstrate prejudice, petitioner would have to show that the physical evidence was kept in conditions that compromised its integrity and that the degraded quality of the evidence resulted in prejudice to petitioner. Petitioner does not make such a claim, nor does he attempt to prove anything of the sort. Trial counsel’s alleged errors with respect to the physical evidence would not have made a difference in the outcome at trial.
Petitioner does not point to evidence to show what counsel would have discovered had he obtained all of the materials in the State’s possession at the time of trial. Perhaps petitioner means to suggest that trial counsel would have cross examined the witnesses more effectively had he possessed a more complete record, but such an interpretation is born in conjecture and speculation. James v. Borg, 24 F.3d 20, 26 (9th Cir.1994) (“Conclusory allegations which are not supported by a statement of specific facts do not warrant habeas relief”)
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(11).
9. Counsel’s failure to have physical evidence tested independently (Claim 5(D)(12))
In Claim 5(D)(12), petitioner alleges that trial counsel failed to obtain an independent evaluation of the State’s testing of the physical evidence, including hair samples, saliva, blood, bite marks, vaginal swabs, and then unreasonably failed to move to exclude that evidence. Petitioner contends that this error both allowed the jury to rely upon false and materially misleading information and prevented trial counsel from moving to exclude 'petitioner’s confession on grounds that it was unreliable and the product of impermissible interrogation tactics by the police.
Petitioner must demonstrate both that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Petitioner cannot prevail on this claim. As with Claim 5(D)(11), the physical evidence corroborated petitioner’s identity, but that was not an issue at trial because petitioner confessed. Petitioner has not shown how trial counsel’s failure to conduct an independent evaluation of the physical evidence caused the jury to rely upon false and materially misleading information or how this alleged occurrence prejudiced him. This speculative allegation cannot support relief. Jones, 66 F.3d at 204-05; cf. Allison, 431 U.S. at 75 n. 7, 97 S.Ct. 1621. Moreover, petitioner’s claim that trial counsel’s failure to conduct an independent evaluation of the evidence prevented trial counsel from moving to exclude petitioner’s confession on grounds that it was unreliable and the product of impermissible interrogation tactics by the police fails. Trial counsel did move to suppress the evidence on these grounds. (Partial Summary Judgment Order (Oct. 2, 1997) at 29-30 (granting summary judgment to respondent on portion of Claim 5(D)(14) concerning trial counsel’s failure to object to petitioner’s confession as unreliable due to coercive police tactics).)
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(12).
10. Counsel’s failure to investigate the reliability of petitioner’s statement (Claim 5(D)(15))
In Claim 5(D)(15), petitioner argues that trial counsel failed to make an inquiry into the reliability of petitioner’s statement to the police that he placed one of his victims on the cowling of the van, given the absence of corroborating evidence in the pathologist’s reports. Petitioner alleges that this error allowed the jury to be subject to false impression testimony.
To prevail on this claim, petitioner must show that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
It is unclear what petitioner is alleging that counsel should have done to inquire into the reliability of petitioner’s statement. Petitioner’s claim also assumes that a tangible injury to Bristol’s body would have resulted and been visible if petitioner in fact placed Bristol’s body on the hot cowling, which may not be the case. Again, conclusory allegations do not support habeas relief. James, 24 F.3d at 26.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(15).
11. Counsel’s failure to adequately cross examine a State witness (Claim 5(D)(16))
In Claim 5(D)(16), petitioner alleges that trial counsel inadequately cross examined a police officer called to testify for the State because trial counsel elicited testimony that the police officer was a polygrapher. Petitioner has withdrawn this claim. Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(16).
12. Counsel’s failure to call Cliff Williams (Claim 5(D)(17))
In Claim 5(D)(17), petitioner contends that trial counsel failed to secure the presence of a critical defense witness, Cliff Williams, at trial.
Officer Williams was a twelve-year veteran of the Long Beach Police Department who retired after being injured in the line of duty. His daughter, Heidi, was engaged to petitioner at the time of the crimes. Trial counsel had interviewed Officer Williams several times before trial. Officer Williams told trial counsel that he liked petitioner and that petitioner had a future if he applied himself. Officer Williams agreed to testify on petitioner’s behalf, and trial counsel told the jury in his penalty phase opening argument that he intended to call Officer Williams. At some point during trial, Officer Williams expressed his concern that testifying for petitioner would have an adverse impact on Officer Williams’s real estate business, which relied in large part on business from Long Beach police officers. Thus, Officer Williams informed trial counsel that he did not want to testify at trial. Although counsel had subpoenaed Officer Williams to testify three times, once for each trial date, trial counsel did not put Officer Williams on the stand.
Again, petitioner must demonstrate that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
Respondent argues that trial counsel made a tactical decision not to call Officer Williams because he had become an unwilling character witness. Petitioner counters, convincingly, that counsel’s failure to call Officer Williams was not tactical, citing numerous times to trial counsel’s eleven-volume deposition transcript. {See, e.g., 9 CDD 27.) Counsel also explained how he tended to use subpoenas:
[I]f I am sure that they are going to testify and they are going to testify, good for me. Then what happens is I don’t bother to subpoena them because they are going to show up and they are going to testify anyway and a subpoena might make them mad. That is not the situation with a police officer.
A police officer may very well — and this is ‘just my experience and it may not match up with anyone else’s — a police officer sometimes has to have an excuse to testify because if he doesn’t have an excuse his fellow officers are going to get angry with him.
So, as a consequence what you do is you give him, no matter how helpful he wants to be and it appears that he is going to be, you put a piece of paper in his hand and say, here, you are subpoenaed and then he has to testify. The other officers understand that and generally that is good enough. That is why they tell the truth.
(9 CDD 48-49.) Trial counsel’s failure to call Officer Williams was neither strategic nor reasonable.
Trial counsel stated in his opening argument that he intended to call three non-family character witnesses during the penalty phase: (1) Kimberly Parker, a friend of petitioner’s; (2) Heidi Williams Brown, petitioner’s fiancée at the time of the crime; and (3) Cliff Williams, Heidi’s father. Trial counsel did in fact call Parker and Brown. Parker testified that petitioner convinced her that she should stop drinking alcohol and smoking marijuana because she had Diabetes. (14 RT 3608-09.) Brown testified that she had been engaged to petitioner, that they had “normal” sexual relations and that the only time he displayed any type of violence was when she once hit him very hard with a pipe, causing welts, and petitioner slapped her in response. (14 RT 3615-16, 3618.) The evidence from both Parker and Brown consumed less than fourteen pages of transcripts, including cross examination. Counsel then failed to call Office Williams, potentially his most effective and credible character witness, despite telling the jury in his opening statement that he intended to put Officer Williams on the stand and even though Officer Williams had been subpoenaed for trial. Counsel’s failure to put Officer Williams on the stand was deficient.
Officer Williams provided the following evidentiary hearing testimony:
I knew Francis Hernandez very well when he was a young man. Francis dated my daughter Heidi. Francis was a good kid, and I had a favorable opinion of him. When Francis was arrested for murder, it was truly a shock for all of us.
Before Francis’s trial, his attorney met with me because he wanted to know what I thought of Francis. I answered Francis’s attorney’s questions truthfully, and the attorney asked me to testify on Francis’s behalf at his murder trial. I agreed to do so, but I later changed my mind. If I had testified at Francis’s trial, or at any trial, I would certainly have told the truth.
(Williams 6/22/03 Decl. at 1, ¶¶ 1-2.)
Again, the prejudice inquiry requires the Court to assess all of the mitigating evidence, both that presented at trial and as part of the evidentiary hearing, and then to reweigh that evidence against the State’s case in aggravation. Williams (Terry), 529 U.S. at 398, 120 S.Ct. 1495. Putting Officer Williams, an experienced police officer injured in the line of duty, on the stand to testify as to petitioner’s character likely would have carried more weight with the jury than the very limited character evidence presented. Officer Williams clearly had strong character evidence to offer. His daughter had been engaged to petitioner just before the crime occurred, and Officer Williams had spent a lot of time with petitioner. Despite petitioner’s brutal murders of other young women, Officer Williams would have testified that he knew petitioner well at the time of the crime, that he had a positive opinion of petitioner and that petitioner had a future if he applied himself. The positive, coherent character testimony from Officer Williams would have bolstered the evidence presented, particularly compared to the thin mitigation case presented. Moreover, the absence of Officer Williams’s testimony after trial counsel specifically told the jury he was going to put Officer Williams on the stand likely had a substantial negative impact on the penalty phase evidence actually presented at trial.
Nevertheless, petitioner has not established a reasonable probability that the testimony of one police officer who held a positive view of petitioner would have caused the jury to vote for life instead of death. Officer Williams’s testimony could not overcome the brutal circumstances of the crime, coupled with petitioner’s callous and unfeeling penalty phase testimony.
Accordingly, the Court DENIES Claim 5(D)(17), but will consider counsel’s deficiency in the cumulative error analysis.
13. Counsel’s failure to object to the admission of prejudicial character evidence (Claim 5(D)(19))
In Claim 5(D)(19), petitioner argues that trial counsel failed to object to the introduction of highly prejudicial character evidence that on the night of Edna Bristol’s murder, petitioner went looking for a homosexual to assault, found one and then beat him up and robbed him. Petitioner further contends that trial counsel failed to object to the prosecutor’s argument based on that testimony.
Again, petitioner must demonstrate that counsel performed deficiently and that this deficient performance prejudiced petitioner. Wiggins, 539 U.S. at 521, 123 S.Ct. 2527.
The portion of the confession to which petitioner objects is the following:
I was driving down the street. I had just — uh—I was drunk. I was in a weird mood and thinking about a lot of things and just decided to go out and find — find myself a homosexual to beat up on, and I just found one. I just got through robbing him for his last ten dollars.
(Confession Transcript at 3.)
Petitioner contends that his assault of the homosexual, an uncharged offense, was admitted as improper character evidence. Cal. Evid.Code § 1101(a). Petitioner argues that none of the exceptions, including motive, opportunity, intent, preparation, plan, knowledge, identity, absence of mistake or accident, applies. Cal. Evid.Code § 1101(b). Arguably, the intent and accident exceptions may apply. Petitioner’s intent to rape and intent to kill were disputed material issues at trial. See People v. Robbins, 45 Cal.3d 867, 248 Cal.Rptr. 172, 178, 755 P.2d 355 (1988) (citing People v. Kelley, 66 Cal.2d 232, 57 Cal.Rptr. 363, 372, 424 P.2d 947 (1967) (“Such evidence is admissible in cases where the proof of defendant’s intent is ambiguous, as when he admits the acts and denies the necessary intent because of mistake or accident.”).) Petitioner said that he had consensual sex with Edna Bristol but also stated that he “guessed maybe [Edna] thought” that he “ha[d] her ... forcibly.” (Confession Transcript at 5.) He confessed that he covered her nose and mouth with a rag but that he “might have held it there too long” and she stopped moving. (Id. at 6.) He thought she was still alive when he left her body at Marshall Junior High. (Id.)
The admissibility of the uncharged conduct “depends on three principal factors: (1) the materiality of the fact sought to be proved or disproved; (2) the tendency of the uncharged crime to prove or disprove the material fact; and (3) the existence of any rule or policy requiring the exclusion of the relevant evidence.” People v. Thompson, 27 Cal.3d 303, 165 Cal.Rptr. 289, 294, 611 P.2d 883 (1980).
First, the evidence in question— here, petitioner’s attack on a homosexual— must be material to the intent issues disputed at trial. Robbins, 248 Cal.Rptr. at 178, 755 P.2d 355. “To be relevant, an uncharged offense must tend logically, naturally and by reasonable inference to prove the issue(s) on which it is offered.” Id. The California Supreme Court has “long recognized that if a person acts similarly in similar situations, he probably harbors the same intent in each instance, and that such prior conduct may be relevant circumstantial evidence of the actor’s most recent intent.” Id. (citation and internal quotation marks omitted). Moreover, “[t]he inference to be drawn is not that the actor is disposed to commit such acts; instead, the inference to be drawn is that, in light of the first event, the actor, at the time of the second event, must have had the intent attributed to him by the prosecution.” Id. Petitioner’s attack on the gay man, just before he picked up Edna Bristol, suggests that petitioner may have been seeking out individuals he perceived as vulnerable in order to take advantage of them. At this level of generality, petitioner’s uncharged conduct does have some relevance to his intent to rape and murder Edna Bristol later that same night.
However, the uncharged offense also must have a tendency to prove intent or disprove accident. In short, the uncharged conduct must be “substantially similar” to the charged offense. Robbins, 248 Cal.Rptr. at 179, 755 P.2d 355. The charged and uncharged conduct must be fairly similar. See, e.g., People v. Carter, 19 Cal.App.4th 1236, 23 Cal.Rptr.2d 888, 894 (1993) (finding killings substantially similar where both victims were homosexual men of about the same age, both met defendant in public places, both went with defendant to more secluded locations where they were robbed and killed, the killings were close together in time, both victims were shot in the head at close range by the same gun, both victim’s cars were ransacked and left in public places, both victims were robbed of their credit cards and defendant used both victim’s credit cards immediately); People v. Delgado, 10 Cal.App.4th 1837, 13 Cal.Rptr.2d 703, 708 (1992) (finding prior uncharged conduct “sufficiently similar” to prove intent where both uncharged and charged conduct involved burglary of a residence through a window, and items taken as repayment for a debt included a VCR in a pillowcase and stolen jewelry).
Here, there is insufficient “similarity in time, place and potential motive” to make petitioner’s testimony that he beat up and robbed a gay man admissible to prove whether petitioner intended to rape and murder Edna Bristol later that night. See People v. Curry, 76 Cal.App.3d 181, 142 Cal.Rptr. 649, 651 (1977). The uncharged conduct does not tend to prove intent to rape or kill. On this basis alone, counsel could have successfully challenged the admission of the uncharged conduct as impermissible character evidence.
Even if the acts were substantially similar, the Court also should consider any policy or rule that would require exclusion of the evidence. For example, “[e]vidence may be excluded under Evidence Code Section 352 if its probative value is ‘substantially outweighed by the probability that its admission would create substantial danger of undue prejudice, of confusing the issues, or of misleading the jury.’ ” People v. Lindberg, 45 Cal.4th 1, 22-23, 82 Cal.Rptr.3d 323, 190 P.3d 664 (2008) (citation and internal quotation marks omitted). In addition, “[b]eeause substantial prejudice is inherent in the case of uncharged offenses, such evidence is admissible only if it has substantial probative value.” Id.
